Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks lately. Mostly around my jaw—like it looks saggy to me, and uneven on one side. It sounds kind of silly saying it out loud.

It really hit me after we were looking at some family photos, and you know how everyone passes the phone around and laughs about who blinked or whatever… but I just kept zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I keep seeing it. It’s hard to shake once I notice it.

Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and meet with clients. It’s not like I’ve stopped everything. But I’ve noticed I’m slower getting out the door for meetings because I’ll get caught up checking how I look, or I’ll be thinking about it during the appointment and not really listening as well as I should.

And socially… my wife and I used to go to these art openings sometimes—she likes them more than I do, honestly, though I do like the free cheese and pretending I understand abstract sculpture—but I’ve skipped a few lately because I just didn’t want to be seen, or have someone take a picture and post it somewhere. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way some.

Yeah… I do, I guess. Before client meetings especially, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.

But then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.

Not drags, she enjoys them, and I’m just there trying not to stand under harsh overhead lights. So… yeah, there’s definitely checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts about your appearance when you're not intentionally thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does pop in when I’m not trying to think about it. Like I’ll just be doing something normal and suddenly I’m back on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially the one side of my face.\n\nIt really started after those family photos, I think. Everyone else was just having fun with them, and I got stuck zooming in like I was doing forensic analysis or something. My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nBut then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.

It got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.

Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks lately. Mostly around my jaw—like it looks saggy to me, and uneven on one side. It sounds kind of silly saying it out loud.\n\nIt really hit me after we were looking at some family photos, and you know how everyone passes the phone around and laughs about who blinked or whatever… but I just kept zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I keep seeing it. It’s hard to shake once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and meet with clients. It’s not like I’ve stopped everything. But I’ve noticed I’m slower getting out the door for meetings because I’ll get caught up checking how I look, or I’ll be thinking about it during the appointment and not really listening as well as I should.\n\nAnd socially… my wife and I used to go to these art openings sometimes—she likes them more than I do, honestly, though I do like the free cheese and pretending I understand abstract sculpture—but I’ve skipped a few lately because I just didn’t want to be seen, or have someone take a picture and post it somewhere. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any repetitive behaviors, like checking mirrors or touching your face, in response to these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Before client meetings especially, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Then I’ll pull out my phone camera too, because the lighting in those office bathrooms is terrible—though honestly, I don’t know why I think my phone camera is going to be kinder, it never is.\n\nI’ll kind of adjust the lighting, turn my face a little, fix my collar, see if holding my jaw a certain way makes it look less… off. And then sometimes I’m in the meeting and I’m not even totally listening because I’m thinking, like, “Does this guy notice?” Or I’ll compare myself to younger advisors, or people at those gallery events my wife drags me to. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. But I’ve noticed I’m slower getting out the door for meetings because I’ll get caught up checking how I look, or I’ll be thinking about it during the appointment and not really listening as well as I should.\n\nAnd socially… my wife and I used to go to these art openings sometimes—she likes them more than I do, honestly, though I do like the free cheese and pretending I understand abstract sculpture—but I’ve skipped a few lately because I just didn’t want to be seen, or have someone take a picture and post it somewhere. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any repetitive behaviors, like checking mirrors or touching your face, in response to these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Before client meetings especially, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Then I’ll pull out my phone camera too, because the lighting in those office bathrooms is terrible—though honestly, I don’t know why I think my phone camera is going to be kinder, it never is.\n\nI’ll kind of adjust the lighting, turn my face a little, fix my collar, see if holding my jaw a certain way makes it look less… off. And then sometimes I’m in the meeting and I’m not even totally listening because I’m thinking, like, “Does this guy notice?” Or I’ll compare myself to younger advisors, or people at those gallery events my wife drags me to. Not drags, she enjoys them, and I’m just there trying not to stand under harsh overhead lights. So… yeah, there’s definitely checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts about your appearance when you're not intentionally thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does pop in when I’m not trying to think about it. Like I’ll just be doing something normal and suddenly I’m back on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially the one side of my face.\n\nIt really started after those family photos, I think. Everyone else was just having fun with them, and I got stuck zooming in like I was doing forensic analysis or something. My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nBut then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you were not concerned about the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.\n\nWhat brings me in is that now I keep getting stuck on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, and one side of my face especially. And the frustrating part is my wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but after seeing myself in all those photos, it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had concerns about other parts of your body, beyond just your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve had normal stuff, like thinking I look tired or that a shirt makes me look heavier—my wife will tell you I can take forever picking something to wear before a client meeting—but that’s not what’s been bothering me.\n\nWhat brings me in is this thing where I keep getting stuck on the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. Ever since those family photos, it’s like my eye goes straight there. And even though my wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to either, I keep circling back to it. So… beyond my face, no, not really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s… I’d say it’s bothering me more than I want to admit, but I’m still functioning. Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.

I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.

But then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.

So… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.

My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I keep seeing it. It’s hard to shake once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. Like I’ll just be doing something normal and suddenly I’m back on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially the one side of my face.\n\nIt really started after those family photos, I think. Everyone else was just having fun with them, and I got stuck zooming in like I was doing forensic analysis or something. My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.

It really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.

So I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.

I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.

But… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.

So, I guess… separate, mostly. But when I’m focused on it, it doesn’t feel very separate.

My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nBut then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you were not concerned about the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.\n\nWhat brings me in is that now I keep getting stuck on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, and one side of my face especially. And the frustrating part is my wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but after seeing myself in all those photos, it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had concerns about other parts of your body, beyond just your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve had normal stuff, like thinking I look tired or that a shirt makes me look heavier—my wife will tell you I can take forever picking something to wear before a client meeting—but that’s not what’s been bothering me.\n\nWhat brings me in is this thing where I keep getting stuck on the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. Ever since those family photos, it’s like my eye goes straight there. And even though my wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to either, I keep circling back to it. So… beyond my face, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your jaw's appearance ever changed to focusing on a different specific flaw or detail over time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s stayed pretty much on that same spot—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nI mean, I can notice other little things if I’m in bad lighting or I catch myself at some weird angle, but that’s more like… passing annoyance. The thing I keep getting stuck on is the jaw. Especially after those family photos—I kept zooming in and thinking, “How long has it looked like that?” Even though my wife says she barely notices, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, my attention just keeps going back there.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your jaw's appearance are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s bothering me more than I want to admit, but I’m still functioning. Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.\n\nBut I notice it slows me down getting out the door, especially before client meetings. I’ll check the mirror, then check again, then I’m thinking about the lighting in their office or whether they’ll be looking at that side of my face. And sometimes during appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which is frustrating because I’m usually good at focusing on people.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings—my wife and I used to go to those more—because I just didn’t want to be seen, or worse, photographed. So… it’s not ruining my whole life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone were to 'fix' the sagging and unevenness around your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jaw was resolved, or would you then find something else to worry about?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nIt really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person is tied to how you look, or is it separate from your appearance concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.\n\nBut… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.\n\nSo, I guess… separate, mostly. But when I’m focused on it, it doesn’t feel very separate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not major things, I guess… I mean, I still go to work. I still meet clients, I still do the meetings and get through the day.

But I have noticed I’m slower getting out the door for client appointments sometimes. I’ll be ready, and then I’ll catch myself in the hall mirror or the car window and think, “Wait, is it really obvious today?” And then I’m fussing around when I should already be leaving.

During some appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which bothers me. Someone will be talking about retirement income or their kids’ college fund, and part of me is wondering if they’re looking at that side of my face. It’s embarrassing to even say that.

And, yeah… I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife likes those, and there are always people taking photos, posting things. I just didn’t want to be seen like that or end up in the background of someone’s picture. So not everything, but some things, yes.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks lately. Mostly around my jaw—like it looks saggy to me, and uneven on one side. It sounds kind of silly saying it out loud.\n\nIt really hit me after we were looking at some family photos, and you know how everyone passes the phone around and laughs about who blinked or whatever… but I just kept zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I keep seeing it. It’s hard to shake once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and meet with clients. It’s not like I’ve stopped everything. But I’ve noticed I’m slower getting out the door for meetings because I’ll get caught up checking how I look, or I’ll be thinking about it during the appointment and not really listening as well as I should.\n\nAnd socially… my wife and I used to go to these art openings sometimes—she likes them more than I do, honestly, though I do like the free cheese and pretending I understand abstract sculpture—but I’ve skipped a few lately because I just didn’t want to be seen, or have someone take a picture and post it somewhere. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any repetitive behaviors, like checking mirrors or touching your face, in response to these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Before client meetings especially, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Then I’ll pull out my phone camera too, because the lighting in those office bathrooms is terrible—though honestly, I don’t know why I think my phone camera is going to be kinder, it never is.\n\nI’ll kind of adjust the lighting, turn my face a little, fix my collar, see if holding my jaw a certain way makes it look less… off. And then sometimes I’m in the meeting and I’m not even totally listening because I’m thinking, like, “Does this guy notice?” Or I’ll compare myself to younger advisors, or people at those gallery events my wife drags me to. Not drags, she enjoys them, and I’m just there trying not to stand under harsh overhead lights. So… yeah, there’s definitely checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts about your appearance when you're not intentionally thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does pop in when I’m not trying to think about it. Like I’ll just be doing something normal and suddenly I’m back on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially the one side of my face.\n\nIt really started after those family photos, I think. Everyone else was just having fun with them, and I got stuck zooming in like I was doing forensic analysis or something. My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nBut then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you were not concerned about the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.\n\nWhat brings me in is that now I keep getting stuck on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, and one side of my face especially. And the frustrating part is my wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but after seeing myself in all those photos, it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had concerns about other parts of your body, beyond just your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve had normal stuff, like thinking I look tired or that a shirt makes me look heavier—my wife will tell you I can take forever picking something to wear before a client meeting—but that’s not what’s been bothering me.\n\nWhat brings me in is this thing where I keep getting stuck on the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. Ever since those family photos, it’s like my eye goes straight there. And even though my wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to either, I keep circling back to it. So… beyond my face, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your jaw's appearance ever changed to focusing on a different specific flaw or detail over time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s stayed pretty much on that same spot—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nI mean, I can notice other little things if I’m in bad lighting or I catch myself at some weird angle, but that’s more like… passing annoyance. The thing I keep getting stuck on is the jaw. Especially after those family photos—I kept zooming in and thinking, “How long has it looked like that?” Even though my wife says she barely notices, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, my attention just keeps going back there.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your jaw's appearance are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s bothering me more than I want to admit, but I’m still functioning. Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.\n\nBut I notice it slows me down getting out the door, especially before client meetings. I’ll check the mirror, then check again, then I’m thinking about the lighting in their office or whether they’ll be looking at that side of my face. And sometimes during appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which is frustrating because I’m usually good at focusing on people.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings—my wife and I used to go to those more—because I just didn’t want to be seen, or worse, photographed. So… it’s not ruining my whole life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone were to 'fix' the sagging and unevenness around your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jaw was resolved, or would you then find something else to worry about?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nIt really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person is tied to how you look, or is it separate from your appearance concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.\n\nBut… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.\n\nSo, I guess… separate, mostly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe. I mean, I keep wanting to say no, because it really feels like it’s this one thing — the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. That’s what I get stuck on.

It got a lot worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. Funny, because everyone else was talking about how nice the pictures were, who was blinking, whether my nephew looked like my brother at that age… and I’m sitting there zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice, at least not that they’ve ever said.

So I don’t know if I’d move on to something else. I’d like to think I wouldn’t. But right now, this jaw thing feels like the main thing I can’t stop criticizing.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks lately. Mostly around my jaw—like it looks saggy to me, and uneven on one side. It sounds kind of silly saying it out loud.\n\nIt really hit me after we were looking at some family photos, and you know how everyone passes the phone around and laughs about who blinked or whatever… but I just kept zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I keep seeing it. It’s hard to shake once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and meet with clients. It’s not like I’ve stopped everything. But I’ve noticed I’m slower getting out the door for meetings because I’ll get caught up checking how I look, or I’ll be thinking about it during the appointment and not really listening as well as I should.\n\nAnd socially… my wife and I used to go to these art openings sometimes—she likes them more than I do, honestly, though I do like the free cheese and pretending I understand abstract sculpture—but I’ve skipped a few lately because I just didn’t want to be seen, or have someone take a picture and post it somewhere. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself performing any repetitive behaviors, like checking mirrors or touching your face, in response to these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I guess. Before client meetings especially, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror more than once. Then I’ll pull out my phone camera too, because the lighting in those office bathrooms is terrible—though honestly, I don’t know why I think my phone camera is going to be kinder, it never is.\n\nI’ll kind of adjust the lighting, turn my face a little, fix my collar, see if holding my jaw a certain way makes it look less… off. And then sometimes I’m in the meeting and I’m not even totally listening because I’m thinking, like, “Does this guy notice?” Or I’ll compare myself to younger advisors, or people at those gallery events my wife drags me to. Not drags, she enjoys them, and I’m just there trying not to stand under harsh overhead lights. So… yeah, there’s definitely checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts about your appearance when you're not intentionally thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does pop in when I’m not trying to think about it. Like I’ll just be doing something normal and suddenly I’m back on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially the one side of my face.\n\nIt really started after those family photos, I think. Everyone else was just having fun with them, and I got stuck zooming in like I was doing forensic analysis or something. My wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t seem to notice, but once the thought shows up, it kind of hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks the way you think it does, or is it more that you're afraid it might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s the hard part. Part of me thinks, yes, it really does look that way—like there’s this sagging, uneven thing around my jaw, especially on one side. It’s what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nBut then my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to react like they see it. So then I think maybe I’m more afraid that it looks that way to other people than it actually does. It got worse after those family photos, because once I saw myself in them, I couldn’t stop seeing it. Like when you notice a scratch on your car and suddenly it’s the only thing you can look at—even though everyone else is talking about the paint color or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face's appearance, like the sagging or unevenness, ever change or shift to different aspects of your face over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on that same thing, honestly—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially that one side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you were not concerned about the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.\n\nWhat brings me in is that now I keep getting stuck on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, and one side of my face especially. And the frustrating part is my wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but after seeing myself in all those photos, it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had concerns about other parts of your body, beyond just your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve had normal stuff, like thinking I look tired or that a shirt makes me look heavier—my wife will tell you I can take forever picking something to wear before a client meeting—but that’s not what’s been bothering me.\n\nWhat brings me in is this thing where I keep getting stuck on the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. Ever since those family photos, it’s like my eye goes straight there. And even though my wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to either, I keep circling back to it. So… beyond my face, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your jaw's appearance ever changed to focusing on a different specific flaw or detail over time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s stayed pretty much on that same spot—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nI mean, I can notice other little things if I’m in bad lighting or I catch myself at some weird angle, but that’s more like… passing annoyance. The thing I keep getting stuck on is the jaw. Especially after those family photos—I kept zooming in and thinking, “How long has it looked like that?” Even though my wife says she barely notices, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, my attention just keeps going back there.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your jaw's appearance are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s bothering me more than I want to admit, but I’m still functioning. Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.\n\nBut I notice it slows me down getting out the door, especially before client meetings. I’ll check the mirror, then check again, then I’m thinking about the lighting in their office or whether they’ll be looking at that side of my face. And sometimes during appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which is frustrating because I’m usually good at focusing on people.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings—my wife and I used to go to those more—because I just didn’t want to be seen, or worse, photographed. So… it’s not ruining my whole life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone were to 'fix' the sagging and unevenness around your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jaw was resolved, or would you then find something else to worry about?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nIt really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person is tied to how you look, or is it separate from your appearance concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.\n\nBut… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.\n\nSo, I guess… separate, mostly. But when I’m focused on it, it doesn’t feel very separate.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw's appearance ever led to avoiding important life events or activities because of fear of being seen or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Not major things, I guess… I mean, I still go to work. Someone will be talking about retirement income or their kids’ college fund, and part of me is wondering if they’re looking at that side of my face. I mean, I can get distracted by other little things if the lighting is awful, like in a dressing room or one of those restaurant bathrooms with the spotlight right over your head—my wife and I went to this place recently where the mirror made everyone look like they hadn’t slept in a week—but what brings me in is really that jaw thing.\n\nIt got a lot worse after those family photos. I kept looking at them and thinking, “Is that what I look like?” And even though my wife says she barely sees it, and clients don’t seem to notice anything, I just keep getting pulled back to that unevenness. So… no, it doesn’t really shift much. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you were not concerned about the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before those family photos, I don’t think I was stuck on it like this. I might’ve noticed bad angles here and there, like anyone does—especially now with phone cameras making you look strange depending on the lens—but it wasn’t this thing I kept coming back to.\n\nWhat brings me in is that now I keep getting stuck on that sagging, uneven look around my jaw, and one side of my face especially. And the frustrating part is my wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but after seeing myself in all those photos, it’s like I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had concerns about other parts of your body, beyond just your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve had normal stuff, like thinking I look tired or that a shirt makes me look heavier—my wife will tell you I can take forever picking something to wear before a client meeting—but that’s not what’s been bothering me.\n\nWhat brings me in is this thing where I keep getting stuck on the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. Ever since those family photos, it’s like my eye goes straight there. And even though my wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to either, I keep circling back to it. So… beyond my face, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your jaw's appearance ever changed to focusing on a different specific flaw or detail over time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s stayed pretty much on that same spot—the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nI mean, I can notice other little things if I’m in bad lighting or I catch myself at some weird angle, but that’s more like… passing annoyance. The thing I keep getting stuck on is the jaw. Especially after those family photos—I kept zooming in and thinking, “How long has it looked like that?” Even though my wife says she barely notices, and clients don’t seem to react or anything, my attention just keeps going back there.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your jaw's appearance are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it’s bothering me more than I want to admit, but I’m still functioning. Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.\n\nBut I notice it slows me down getting out the door, especially before client meetings. I’ll check the mirror, then check again, then I’m thinking about the lighting in their office or whether they’ll be looking at that side of my face. And sometimes during appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which is frustrating because I’m usually good at focusing on people.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings—my wife and I used to go to those more—because I just didn’t want to be seen, or worse, photographed. So… it’s not ruining my whole life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone were to 'fix' the sagging and unevenness around your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jaw was resolved, or would you then find something else to worry about?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nIt really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person is tied to how you look, or is it separate from your appearance concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.\n\nBut… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.\n\nSo, I guess… separate, mostly. But when I’m focused on it, it doesn’t feel very separate.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw's appearance ever led to avoiding important life events or activities because of fear of being seen or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Not major things, I guess… I mean, I still go to work. I still meet clients, I still do the meetings and get through the day.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m slower getting out the door for client appointments sometimes. I’ll be ready, and then I’ll catch myself in the hall mirror or the car window and think, “Wait, is it really obvious today?” And then I’m fussing around when I should already be leaving.\n\nDuring some appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which bothers me. Someone will be talking about retirement income or their kids’ college fund, and part of me is wondering if they’re looking at that side of my face. It’s embarrassing to even say that.\n\nAnd, yeah… I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife likes those, and there are always people taking photos, posting things. I just didn’t want to be seen like that or end up in the background of someone’s picture. So not everything, but some things, yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still find things to criticize about your appearance even if the issue with your jaw was completely resolved?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean, I keep wanting to say no, because it really feels like it’s this one thing — the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nIt got a lot worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. Funny, because everyone else was talking about how nice the pictures were, who was blinking, whether my nephew looked like my brother at that age… and I’m sitting there zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice, at least not that they’ve ever said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d move on to something else. I’d like to think I wouldn’t. But right now, this jaw thing feels like the main thing I can’t stop criticizing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… I keep circling back to the jaw, honestly. That’s what brings me in. It’s this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face, and once I saw it in those family photos, I couldn’t really unsee it.

I mean, everyone else was talking about normal family-photo stuff, like who looked good or who had their eyes closed, and I was just stuck zooming in on that one side of my face. My wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t seem to notice either, but it still feels obvious to me.

So if it were completely fixed… I’d like to think I wouldn’t just find something else. But I guess I can’t say for sure. Right now it feels very specifically about that jaw area.

Like, I’m still going to work, still meeting clients, still doing what I need to do.\n\nBut I notice it slows me down getting out the door, especially before client meetings. So… it’s not ruining my whole life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone were to 'fix' the sagging and unevenness around your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think if someone fixed that sagging, uneven part around my jaw, I’d stop worrying about it. That’s the thing that keeps pulling my attention.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’d still check, you know? Like after those family photos, I just started seeing it everywhere—mirrors, pictures, the little reflection in a window, which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about clients’ portfolios, not my jawline. My wife says she barely sees anything, and clients don’t seem to notice, but I keep coming back to that one side of my face.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel better if it were fixed. I guess I’m not totally sure I’d trust it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this one issue with your jaw was resolved, or would you then find something else to worry about?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, I want to say I’d be satisfied if that one jaw thing was fixed. That’s what brings me in, really — I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face.\n\nIt really kicked up after those family photos. You know how everyone takes a million pictures now, and then they get shared around, and you see yourself from angles you never asked for? I kept noticing it in those, even though my wife says she barely sees anything. Clients don’t seem to notice either, at least not that they’ve said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d find something else. Maybe not. It feels like it’s this specific thing that keeps pulling me back. But I guess I can’t say for sure until it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person is tied to how you look, or is it separate from your appearance concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, like my worth as a person is tied to it. I mean, I know I’m more than my jawline or whatever. I have a job, a family, people who rely on me.\n\nBut… appearances do matter in some ways, don’t they? Especially with clients. Not that they’re judging my jaw specifically, but you want to look put together. I had a client once who came in wearing this really expensive watch but his documents were a disaster, and I remember thinking people notice the surface first, even if that’s not the important part.\n\nSo, I guess… separate, mostly. But when I’m focused on it, it doesn’t feel very separate.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw's appearance ever led to avoiding important life events or activities because of fear of being seen or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Not major things, I guess… I mean, I still go to work. I still meet clients, I still do the meetings and get through the day.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m slower getting out the door for client appointments sometimes. I’ll be ready, and then I’ll catch myself in the hall mirror or the car window and think, “Wait, is it really obvious today?” And then I’m fussing around when I should already be leaving.\n\nDuring some appointments I’m not as present as I should be, which bothers me. Someone will be talking about retirement income or their kids’ college fund, and part of me is wondering if they’re looking at that side of my face. It’s embarrassing to even say that.\n\nAnd, yeah… I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife likes those, and there are always people taking photos, posting things. I just didn’t want to be seen like that or end up in the background of someone’s picture. So not everything, but some things, yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still find things to criticize about your appearance even if the issue with your jaw was completely resolved?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean, I keep wanting to say no, because it really feels like it’s this one thing — the sagging, uneven look around my jaw, especially one side of my face. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nIt got a lot worse after seeing myself in all those family photos. Funny, because everyone else was talking about how nice the pictures were, who was blinking, whether my nephew looked like my brother at that age… and I’m sitting there zooming in on my face. My wife says she barely notices it, and clients don’t seem to notice, at least not that they’ve ever said.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d move on to something else. I’d like to think I wouldn’t. But right now, this jaw thing feels like the main thing I can’t stop criticizing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still find flaws in your appearance even if the issue with your jaw was completely fixed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I keep circling back to the jaw, honestly. That’s what brings me in. It’s this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face, and once I saw it in those family photos, I couldn’t really unsee it.\n\nI mean, everyone else was talking about normal family-photo stuff, like who looked good or who had their eyes closed, and I was just stuck zooming in on that one side of my face. Right now it feels very specifically about that jaw area.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think people are actually noticing the sagging and unevenness around your jaw, or is it more that you're afraid they might notice it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / personal_financial_advisor / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face, especially after seeing myself in all those family photos, even though my wife and clients say they barely notice anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I’m afraid they might notice it… although in the moment it feels like they are noticing it.

Like with clients, nobody has said anything, and my wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about. But I’ll catch myself thinking they’re looking at that side of my face, the sagging or uneven part around my jaw.

It really got stuck in my head after those family photos. I mean, everyone else was just looking at the pictures normally, and I kept zooming in on my face like I was checking a financial statement line by line or something. So… I guess I don’t have proof people notice. It’s more that I can’t stop noticing it, and then I assume they might too.
